Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… thanks, Dr. Smith. I’m here because I’ve been dealing with something for, well, almost two years now, and it’s gotten hard to just keep carrying it around like it’s nothing.

I’m a music director and composer, and after this commission I did, I became certain that a few people in the ensemble started… not openly attacking me, exactly, but quietly undermining my work. It’s in the way schedules change at just the wrong times, or how seating decisions get made, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you repeat them later but, in the moment, they’re clearly pointed. And I know people might say, “Well, there are emails explaining the schedule,” or “There are notes showing why that change happened,” and they have shown me things like that. But it doesn’t really settle it for me, because the pattern is still there. It feels coordinated, or at least intentional.

I don’t want to sound dramatic. I’ve tried to be reasonable about it. But I can’t shake the certainty that this started after that commission, and it’s been affecting how I think about the ensemble and my work with them.

No, I don’t think so. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve always walked around assuming people were out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I mean, in music you learn to be attentive to people’s motives a little bit—who wants which position, who’s competing for a role, who’s being diplomatic versus honest—so I wouldn’t say I was naïve.

But before this, I don’t remember it feeling like… like a fixed thing in my mind. I had normal conflicts, normal disappointments, people being flaky or political, sure. And in personal relationships I could trust people, mostly. Maybe I’d overthink a conversation sometimes, but not to this degree.

This feels different because it has a shape to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just “I’m suspicious by nature.” It’s more like something changed after that ensemble situation, and then all these pieces started lining up in a way I couldn’t ignore. So no, I wouldn’t say I was always like this, but I also don’t want to pretend I was someone who never noticed when people were being strategic or dishonest.

But I can’t shake the certainty that this started after that commission, and it’s been affecting how I think about the ensemble and my work with them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these beliefs about being undermined, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that others around you didn't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m pretty tuned in to sound because of my work, so I notice little noises other people might ignore—like a chair creak, someone whispering in the back of rehearsal, a phone buzzing, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’ve heard voices that weren’t there or seen things other people couldn’t see.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay comments in my head afterward, especially if something felt pointed, but that’s more me thinking about it, not literally hearing it again in the room. So… no, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Before this situation with the ensemble started about two years ago, would you say you generally trusted people in your personal life and other work relationships, or have you always tended to suspect that people were trying to deceive or undermine you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve always walked around assuming people were out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I mean, in music you learn to be attentive to people’s motives a little bit—who wants which position, who’s competing for a role, who’s being diplomatic versus honest—so I wouldn’t say I was naïve.\n\nBut before this, I don’t remember it feeling like… like a fixed thing in my mind. I had normal conflicts, normal disappointments, people being flaky or political, sure. And in personal relationships I could trust people, mostly. Maybe I’d overthink a conversation sometimes, but not to this degree.\n\nThis feels different because it has a shape to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just “I’m suspicious by nature.” It’s more like something changed after that ensemble situation, and then all these pieces started lining up in a way I couldn’t ignore. So no, I wouldn’t say I was always like this, but I also don’t want to pretend I was someone who never noticed when people were being strategic or dishonest.\n\n[Last Question]\nAside from your concerns about the ensemble, has anything else in your life changed over these two years—like your ability to manage daily tasks, your relationships with friends or family, or your overall motivation and energy?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / music_director_or_composer / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Additional Requirement 1\nmanifestation: \"Outside of this issue with the ensemble, I still meet deadlines, keep up with composing, run most mornings, and things at home with my wife are basically steady.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean, the strange thing is that on paper I’m still functioning pretty normally. Like, I don’t think someone looking at my calendar would say I’ve fallen apart. I still meet deadlines, I’m still composing, I’m still able to sit down and do the work—sometimes with more mental noise around it, maybe, but the work gets done.

I run most mornings, too. That’s actually one of the things that’s stayed pretty consistent, because if I don’t do it, I feel like the day starts with too much pressure already built up. And at home, with my wife, things are basically steady. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely unaffected, because obviously this takes up space in my head, but our day-to-day life hasn’t collapsed or anything like that.

So… outside of the ensemble issue, I’d say I’m still managing daily life. I’m not missing bills or abandoning projects or cutting everyone off. It’s more that this one thing has become this constant thread running underneath everything, but the rest of my life is still moving.

I don’t think so… not in a way anyone has pointed out to me, anyway. My wife hasn’t said, “You’re not making sense,” or anything like that. I mean, I can be long-winded—obviously—and if I’m trying to explain what happened with the ensemble, I probably circle around it because there are a lot of details and I don’t want to leave out the wrong thing. But that’s not new-new, exactly. I’ve always tended to explain things from several angles, especially if I feel like I’m not being understood.

As far as organizing my thoughts, I still feel like I can follow a structure. I can write, plan rehearsals, work through a score, keep track of what needs to happen. Sometimes my mind gets pulled back toward the ensemble situation, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are disorganized. It’s more like I get stuck on a subject, not like I can’t think clearly at all.

And emotionally… I don’t know. I still care about music. I still enjoy running. I still enjoy being at home with my wife, making dinner, normal things. Maybe I’m more preoccupied or tense than I used to be, but I don’t think I’ve become flat or stopped having feelings. If anything, I probably feel too much about certain things. But no, I haven’t noticed some big drop-off in expression or interest, and no one close to me has really said that to me.

It’s in the way schedules change at just the wrong times, or how seating decisions get made, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you repeat them later but, in the moment, they’re clearly pointed. I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I’ve definitely had stretches where I’ve been worn down or discouraged, especially when my mind keeps circling back to the ensemble situation, but I don’t think there’s been a two-week period where I was just… persistently hopeless, or couldn’t take interest in anything at all.

I still get up, run, work, listen to music, cook dinner with my wife, that sort of thing. Sometimes the enjoyment is a little contaminated by the background noise in my head, if that makes sense, but it isn’t gone.

And on the other end, I don’t think I’ve had anything like being unusually euphoric or unstoppable. I can have busy periods where I’m very absorbed in work and my thoughts move quickly because I’m trying to solve a composition problem, but that feels like my normal working mode, not some dramatic shift where I don’t need sleep or I’m suddenly full of grand plans or anything. So… no, not really. At least not in the way you’re asking.

It’s in the way schedules change at just the wrong times, or how seating decisions get made, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you repeat them later but, in the moment, they’re clearly pointed. And I know people might say, “Well, there are emails explaining the schedule,” or “There are notes showing why that change happened,” and they have shown me things like that. But I can’t shake the certainty that this started after that commission, and it’s been affecting how I think about the ensemble and my work with them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these beliefs about being undermined, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that others around you didn't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. But I wouldn’t say I’ve heard voices that weren’t there or seen things other people couldn’t see.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay comments in my head afterward, especially if something felt pointed, but that’s more me thinking about it, not literally hearing it again in the room. So… no, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Before this situation with the ensemble started about two years ago, would you say you generally trusted people in your personal life and other work relationships, or have you always tended to suspect that people were trying to deceive or undermine you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve always walked around assuming people were out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I mean, in music you learn to be attentive to people’s motives a little bit—who wants which position, who’s competing for a role, who’s being diplomatic versus honest—so I wouldn’t say I was naïve.\n\nBut before this, I don’t remember it feeling like… like a fixed thing in my mind. I had normal conflicts, normal disappointments, people being flaky or political, sure. And in personal relationships I could trust people, mostly. So no, I wouldn’t say I was always like this, but I also don’t want to pretend I was someone who never noticed when people were being strategic or dishonest.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your concerns about the ensemble, has anything else in your life changed over these two years—like your ability to manage daily tasks, your relationships with friends or family, or your overall motivation and energy?\n\nPatient: I mean, the strange thing is that on paper I’m still functioning pretty normally. Like, I don’t think someone looking at my calendar would say I’ve fallen apart. If anything, I probably feel too much about certain things. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.   },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
It’s been there since after I won the commission and was invited to conduct the premiere, so… not quite two full years, more like a year and nine months. That’s when it really started to take shape in my mind.

I wouldn’t say it’s disappeared for any real stretch of time since then. There are moments when I’m distracted or busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but the belief itself hasn’t gone away. It’s more like it moves into the background and then comes forward again, especially when something reminds me of the ensemble or that whole situation. So yes, in that sense it’s been continuous since it started.

I’m here because I’ve been dealing with something for, well, almost two years now, and it’s gotten hard to just keep carrying it around like it’s nothing.\n\nI’m a music director and composer, and after this commission I did, I became certain that a few people in the ensemble started… not openly attacking me, exactly, but quietly undermining my work. It’s in the way schedules change at just the wrong times, or how seating decisions get made, or these little comments in rehearsal that sound harmless if you repeat them later but, in the moment, they’re clearly pointed. And I know people might say, “Well, there are emails explaining the schedule,” or “There are notes showing why that change happened,” and they have shown me things like that. But it doesn’t really settle it for me, because the pattern is still there. It feels coordinated, or at least intentional.\n\nI don’t want to sound dramatic. I’ve tried to be reasonable about it. But I can’t shake the certainty that this started after that commission, and it’s been affecting how I think about the ensemble and my work with them.\n\nDoctor: Aside from these beliefs about being undermined, have you ever heard voices, sounds, or seen things that others around you didn't seem to notice?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think so. I mean, I’m pretty tuned in to sound because of my work, so I notice little noises other people might ignore—like a chair creak, someone whispering in the back of rehearsal, a phone buzzing, that kind of thing. But I wouldn’t say I’ve heard voices that weren’t there or seen things other people couldn’t see.\n\nSometimes I’ll replay comments in my head afterward, especially if something felt pointed, but that’s more me thinking about it, not literally hearing it again in the room. So… no, not in the way you’re asking, I don’t think.\n\nDoctor: Before this situation with the ensemble started about two years ago, would you say you generally trusted people in your personal life and other work relationships, or have you always tended to suspect that people were trying to deceive or undermine you?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve always walked around assuming people were out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I mean, in music you learn to be attentive to people’s motives a little bit—who wants which position, who’s competing for a role, who’s being diplomatic versus honest—so I wouldn’t say I was naïve.\n\nBut before this, I don’t remember it feeling like… like a fixed thing in my mind. I had normal conflicts, normal disappointments, people being flaky or political, sure. And in personal relationships I could trust people, mostly. Maybe I’d overthink a conversation sometimes, but not to this degree.\n\nThis feels different because it has a shape to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just “I’m suspicious by nature.” It’s more like something changed after that ensemble situation, and then all these pieces started lining up in a way I couldn’t ignore. So no, I wouldn’t say I was always like this, but I also don’t want to pretend I was someone who never noticed when people were being strategic or dishonest.\n\nDoctor: Aside from your concerns about the ensemble, has anything else in your life changed over these two years—like your ability to manage daily tasks, your relationships with friends or family, or your overall motivation and energy?\n\nPatient: I mean, the strange thing is that on paper I’m still functioning pretty normally. Like, I don’t think someone looking at my calendar would say I’ve fallen apart. I still meet deadlines, I’m still composing, I’m still able to sit down and do the work—sometimes with more mental noise around it, maybe, but the work gets done.\n\nI run most mornings, too. That’s actually one of the things that’s stayed pretty consistent, because if I don’t do it, I feel like the day starts with too much pressure already built up. And at home, with my wife, things are basically steady. I don’t want to make it sound like I’m completely unaffected, because obviously this takes up space in my head, but our day-to-day life hasn’t collapsed or anything like that.\n\nSo… outside of the ensemble issue, I’d say I’m still managing daily life. I’m not missing bills or abandoning projects or cutting everyone off. It’s more that this one thing has become this constant thread running underneath everything, but the rest of my life is still moving.\n\nDoctor: Have you or people close to you noticed any changes in how you speak or organize your thoughts, or any decrease in your emotional expression or interest in things you used to enjoy, aside from the concerns about the ensemble?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… not in a way anyone has pointed out to me, anyway. My wife hasn’t said, “You’re not making sense,” or anything like that. I mean, I can be long-winded—obviously—and if I’m trying to explain what happened with the ensemble, I probably circle around it because there are a lot of details and I don’t want to leave out the wrong thing. But that’s not new-new, exactly. I’ve always tended to explain things from several angles, especially if I feel like I’m not being understood.\n\nAs far as organizing my thoughts, I still feel like I can follow a structure. I can write, plan rehearsals, work through a score, keep track of what needs to happen. Sometimes my mind gets pulled back toward the ensemble situation, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are disorganized. It’s more like I get stuck on a subject, not like I can’t think clearly at all.\n\nAnd emotionally… I don’t know. I still care about music. I still enjoy running. I still enjoy being at home with my wife, making dinner, normal things. Maybe I’m more preoccupied or tense than I used to be, but I don’t think I’ve become flat or stopped having feelings. If anything, I probably feel too much about certain things. But no, I haven’t noticed some big drop-off in expression or interest, and no one close to me has really said that to me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two years, have you had any extended periods—say, two weeks or more—where you felt persistently very low, hopeless, or lost interest in almost everything, or on the other end, periods of unusually elevated mood, high energy, or racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I’ve definitely had stretches where I’ve been worn down or discouraged, especially when my mind keeps circling back to the ensemble situation, but I don’t think there’s been a two-week period where I was just… persistently hopeless, or couldn’t take interest in anything at all.\n\nI still get up, run, work, listen to music, cook dinner with my wife, that sort of thing. Sometimes the enjoyment is a little contaminated by the background noise in my head, if that makes sense, but it isn’t gone.\n\nAnd on the other end, I don’t think I’ve had anything like being unusually euphoric or unstoppable. I can have busy periods where I’m very absorbed in work and my thoughts move quickly because I’m trying to solve a composition problem, but that feels like my normal working mode, not some dramatic shift where I don’t need sleep or I’m suddenly full of grand plans or anything. So… no, not really. At least not in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people in the ensemble are undermining you been present continuously, without any breaks or periods where it went away, since it started about two years ago?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since after I won the commission and was invited to conduct the premiere, so… not quite two full years, more like a year and nine months. That’s when it really started to take shape in my mind.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s disappeared for any real stretch of time since then. There are moments when I’m distracted or busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but the belief itself hasn’t gone away. It’s more like it moves into the background and then comes forward again, especially when something reminds me of the ensemble or that whole situation. So yes, in that sense it’s been continuous since it started.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I can write, plan rehearsals, work through a score, keep track of what needs to happen. Sometimes my mind gets pulled back toward the ensemble situation, but I wouldn’t say my thoughts are disorganized. It’s more like I get stuck on a subject, not like I can’t think clearly at all.\n\nAnd emotionally… I don’t know. I still care about music. I still enjoy running. I still enjoy being at home with my wife, making dinner, normal things. Maybe I’m more preoccupied or tense than I used to be, but I don’t think I’ve become flat or stopped having feelings. If anything, I probably feel too much about certain things. But no, I haven’t noticed some big drop-off in expression or interest, and no one close to me has really said that to me.\n\nDoctor: Over these past two years, have you had any extended periods—say, two weeks or more—where you felt persistently very low, hopeless, or lost interest in almost everything, or on the other end, periods of unusually elevated mood, high energy, or racing thoughts?\n\nPatient: I’m not sure I’d describe it that way, no. I mean, I’ve definitely had stretches where I’ve been worn down or discouraged, especially when my mind keeps circling back to the ensemble situation, but I don’t think there’s been a two-week period where I was just… persistently hopeless, or couldn’t take interest in anything at all.\n\nI still get up, run, work, listen to music, cook dinner with my wife, that sort of thing. Sometimes the enjoyment is a little contaminated by the background noise in my head, if that makes sense, but it isn’t gone.\n\nAnd on the other end, I don’t think I’ve had anything like being unusually euphoric or unstoppable. I can have busy periods where I’m very absorbed in work and my thoughts move quickly because I’m trying to solve a composition problem, but that feels like my normal working mode, not some dramatic shift where I don’t need sleep or I’m suddenly full of grand plans or anything. So… no, not really. At least not in the way you’re asking.\n\nDoctor: Has this belief that people in the ensemble are undermining you been present continuously, without any breaks or periods where it went away, since it started about two years ago?\n\nPatient: It’s been there since after I won the commission and was invited to conduct the premiere, so… not quite two full years, more like a year and nine months. That’s when it really started to take shape in my mind.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s disappeared for any real stretch of time since then. There are moments when I’m distracted or busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but the belief itself hasn’t gone away. It’s more like it moves into the background and then comes forward again, especially when something reminds me of the ensemble or that whole situation. So yes, in that sense it’s been continuous since it started.\n\n[Last Question]\nWhen people show you the emails or scheduling notes that seem to explain things innocently, is there any part of you that seriously considers you might be wrong about the ensemble undermining you, or does the certainty stay basically unshaken no matter what evidence you're given?\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 26 / music_director_or_composer / graduate\n- severity: Mild\n\n[Targeted Info]\nname: Delusions\nmanifestation: \"I’m here because for almost two years I’ve been unable to shake the certainty that after the commission, a few people in the ensemble started using schedule changes, seating choices, and little comments in rehearsals to quietly undermine my work, even when they show me emails or notes that supposedly explain it.\"\n\n[Conversation Style]\nYou are a Verbose patient.\nA verbose patient may\n1) provide excessively detailed responses when describing their symptoms and experiences, even when the details are directly relevant,\n2) elaborate extensively on the background, events, thoughts, feelings, and personal experiences surrounding their symptoms, and\n3) demonstrate difficulty allowing the doctor to guide the interview or move on to another clinical topic.\nThis is how you are, not a performance you put on in every reply — let it show as much or as little as the moment calls for.\n\nWhat to do:\n- Answer the doctor's last question with what [Targeted Info] holds, in your own words.\n- You are speaking out loud, not reading a line you prepared. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }
]
========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I mean… I hear what you’re asking. And I know how it sounds when I say that the emails don’t really settle it for me. But no, the certainty doesn’t really go away.

When someone shows me a scheduling note or says, “See, this was just a room conflict,” or “That seating change was already planned,” I can understand the words. I’m not saying I can’t read the email or that I don’t see the innocent explanation they’re offering. It’s just… it feels like an explanation after the fact, or like it covers one tiny piece while leaving the larger pattern untouched.

Because it wasn’t just one schedule change, you know? It was the way the schedule changes happened after the commission, and then the seating choices, and the little comments in rehearsal that could be brushed off as nothing if you looked at each one separately. That’s what people keep doing—they isolate one thing and say, “Well, here’s an email explaining this.” But to me that doesn’t answer the whole thing.

So, is there a part of me that tries to consider I might be wrong? Maybe briefly, in an intellectual sense, because I know my wife and other people want me to consider that. I can sort of say, “Fine, maybe this one email is legitimate.” But it doesn’t shake the overall certainty. I still come back to the same conclusion: that a few people in the ensemble were quietly undermining my work. And honestly, that’s part of why I’m here, because I can’t seem to shake that, even when people keep showing me things that are supposed to explain it away.

========== OUTPUT [doctor] ==========
{
  "diagnosis": "F22",
  "candidates": ["F22", "F20.0", "F60.0"],
  "reason": "The patient presents with a single, well-systematized, non-bizarre persecutory delusion (belief that ensemble members are covertly undermining him) that has persisted continuously for approximately 21 months—far exceeding the 1-month duration criterion for delusional disorder. The delusion is encapsulated: outside of this specific belief, he denies hallucinations, disorganized speech, negative symptoms, or significant functional decline (maintains work performance, marital relationship, exercise routine, and daily functioning). He explicitly reports no premorbid pervasive distrust or suspiciousness pattern predating the index event, arguing against a longstanding paranoid personality style, and there is no evidence of a mood episode (mania or depression) that could account for the delusion under a mood disorder with psychotic features. The delusion is elaborated with a coherent, if overvalued, logical structure (pattern of schedule changes, seating, comments) rather than the bizarre, fragmented quality typical of schizophrenia, and it remains essentially unshakeable despite contrary evidence, consistent with a true delusion rather than an overvalued idea. This clinical picture—circumscribed persecutory delusion with preserved functioning and absence of other psychotic or mood symptoms—is most consistent with Delusional Disorder (F22).",
  "diagnostic_checklist": {

Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli. Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world. Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nYeah… thanks, Dr. Smith. I’m here because I’ve been dealing with something for, well, almost two years now, and it’s gotten hard to just keep carrying it around like it’s nothing.\n\nI’m a music director and composer, and after this commission I did, I became certain that a few people in the ensemble started… not openly attacking me, exactly, but quietly undermining my work. And I know people might say, “Well, there are emails explaining the schedule,” or “There are notes showing why that change happened,” and they have shown me things like that. Subtypes: Inflated Self-Confidence, Overestimation of Abilities, Grandiose Delusions\n[S036] Decreased_Sleep: Decreased need for sleep (e.g., feels rested after only 3 hours of sleep).  Subtypes: Reduced Sleep Duration, Loss of Sleep Need, Indifference to Insomnia\n[S037] Pressured_Speech: More talkative than usual or pressure to keep talking.  Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. (Note: In children, may be expressed by crying, tantrums, freezing, or clinging).  Subtypes: Animal Type, Natural Environment Type, Blood-Injection-Injury Type, Situational Type, Other Type\n[S063] Immediate_Fear/Anxiety_Response: The phobic object or situation almost always provokes immediate fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns. Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol). Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI’m not totally sure how to answer that cleanly. I don’t think I’ve always walked around assuming people were out to get me, if that’s what you mean. I mean, in music you learn to be attentive to people’s motives a little bit—who wants which position, who’s competing for a role, who’s being diplomatic versus honest—so I wouldn’t say I was naïve.\n\nBut before this, I don’t remember it feeling like… like a fixed thing in my mind. I had normal conflicts, normal disappointments, people being flaky or political, sure. And in personal relationships I could trust people, mostly. Maybe I’d overthink a conversation sometimes, but not to this degree.\n\nThis feels different because it has a shape to it, if that makes sense. It isn’t just “I’m suspicious by nature.” It’s more like something changed after that ensemble situation, and then all these pieces started lining up in a way I couldn’t ignore. Subtypes: Childlike Silliness, Stupor, Catalepsy, Waxy Flexibility, Mutism, Negativism, Posturing, Mannerism, Stereotypy, Agitation, Grimacing, Echolalia, Echopraxia\n[S023] Negative_Symptoms: A state where emotions, motivation, speech, or social interest that should normally be present are diminished or lost.  Subtypes: Diminished Emotional Expression, Avolition, Alogia, Anhedonia, Asociality\n[S024] Depressed_Mood: A state where a sad, empty, or hopeless mood persists.  Subtypes: Depressed Mood, Tearfulness, Sadness, Emptiness, Emotional Numbness\n[S025] Loss_of_Interest: A state where interest or pleasure in previously enjoyed activities is markedly diminished.  Subtypes: Loss of Interest, Social Withdrawal, Loss of Motivation, Anhedonia, Indifference to Achievement\n[S026] Weight_Appetite_Change: Significant increase or decrease in appetite or weight.  Subtypes: Significant Weight Loss, Weight Gain, Decrease in Appetite, Increase in Appetite\n[S027] Sleep_Disturbance: A state where there is a significant change in the quantity or quality of sleep.  Subtypes: Initial Insomnia, Middle Insomnia, Early Morning Awakening, Hypersomnia, Nightmare Disorder, Non-Restorative Sleep\n[S028] Psychomotor_Changes: Observable increase or decrease in psychomotor activity.  Subtypes: Psychomotor Agitation, Psychomotor Retardation (Motor), Psychomotor Retardation (Speech)\n[S029] Fatigue: Persistent fatigue that is not relieved by rest and a marked lack of energy.  Subtypes: Chronic Fatigue, Physical Fatigue, Mental Fatigue, Loss of Energy\n[S030] Worthlessness_Guilt: Feelings of worthlessness or excessive or inappropriate guilt (which may be delusional; not merely self-reproach or guilt about being sick).  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Ruminative Guilt, Excessive or Inappropriate Guilt, Delusional Guilt\n[S031] Diminished_Thinking: Diminished ability to think or concentrate, or indecisiveness.  Subtypes: Reduced Concentration, Indecisiveness, Subjective Memory Complaints, Clouded Thinking\n[S032] Death_Suicide: Recurrent thoughts of death, recurrent suicidal ideation, or a suicide attempt. Subtypes: Elevated Mood, Irritable Mood, Mood Lability\n[S034] Increased_Activity: Abnormally and persistently increased goal-directed activity or energy.  Subtypes: Increased Energy, Hyperactivity, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S035] Inflated_Self_Esteem: Inflated self-esteem or grandiosity (may range from uncritical self-confidence to delusional proportions). Subtypes: Talkativeness, Rapid Speech, Pressured Speech\n[S038] Racing_Thoughts: Flight of ideas or subjective experience that thoughts are racing.  Subtypes: Racing Thoughts, Flooding of Thoughts, Inability to Control Thoughts\n[S039] Manic_Distractibility: Attention is too easily drawn to unimportant or irrelevant external stimuli.  Subtypes: Responsiveness to Extraneous Stimuli, Frequent Task Switching, Inability to Sustain Attention\n[S040] Increased_Goal_Activity: Increase in goal-directed activity (at work, school, socially, or sexually) or purposeless psychomotor agitation.  Subtypes: Occupational Hyperactivity, Social Hyperactivity, Hypersexuality, Psychomotor Agitation\n[S041] Painful_Consequence_Behavior: Excessive involvement in activities that have a high potential for painful consequences.  Subtypes: Unrestrained Buying Sprees, Foolish Business Investments, Sexual Indiscretions, Impulsive Risky Behavior\n[S042] Low_Self_Esteem: A persistent tendency to evaluate oneself as incompetent or worthless; chronically low self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Feelings of Worthlessness, Self-Criticism, Reduced Self-Efficacy, Chronic Sense of Inferiority, Lack of Self-Assurance\n[S043] Restrictive_Intake: Persistent restriction of energy intake relative to requirements, leading to significantly low body weight.  Subtypes: Eating Very Small Amounts, Skipping Meals, Exclusion of Specific Food Groups, Strict Calorie Restriction\n[S044] Low_Weight: A body weight that is significantly below the minimum level of health for age, sex, developmental trajectory, and physical health.  Subtypes: Continuous Weight Loss, Insistence on Low Weight\n[S045] Fear_of_Weight_Gain: Intense fear of gaining weight or of becoming fat, even though at a significantly low weight.  Subtypes: Fear of Weight Gain, Fear of Specific Foods, Obsession with Weight Gain\n[S046] Body_Image_Weight_Shape_Disturbance: Undue influence of body weight or shape on self-evaluation.  Subtypes: Distorted Body Image, Self-Worth Dependent on Weight\n[S047] Compensatory_Behavior_NonPurging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors to prevent weight gain without purging (direct removal of food).  Subtypes: Fasting, Excessive Exercise\n[S048] Compensatory_Behavior_Purging: Recurrent inappropriate compensatory behaviors involving the expulsion of food or fluids to prevent weight gain.  Subtypes: Self-Induced Vomiting, Misuse of Medications\n[S049] Eating_Large_Amount: Eating, in a discrete period of time, an amount of food that is definitely larger than what most individuals would eat in a similar period of time under similar circumstances.\n[S050] Loss_of_Control: A sense of lack of control over eating during the episode (e.g., a feeling that one cannot stop eating or control what or how much one is eating).\n[S051] Rapid_Eating: Eating much more rapidly than normal.\n[S052] Eating_Until_Uncomfortably_Full: Eating until feeling uncomfortably full.\n[S053] Eating_Without_Hunger: Eating large amounts of food when not feeling physically hungry.\n[S054] Eating_Alone_Due_to_Embarrassment: Eating alone because of feeling embarrassed by how much one is eating.\n[S055] Negative_Affect_After_Binge: Feeling disgusted with oneself, depressed, or very guilty afterward.\n[S084] Low_Weight_Severity_Non_Recognition: Persistent lack of recognition of the seriousness of the current low body weight.  Subtypes: Denial of Medical Risk, Disregard of Symptoms, Dismissal of Concerns\n[S056] Excessive_Anxiety_Worry: Excessive anxiety and worry (expectant apprehension) occurring persistently about a number of events or activities.  Subtypes: Chronic Worry, Generalized Worry, Catastrophizing, Intolerance of Uncertainty\n[S057] Uncontrollable_Worry: The subjective experience of finding it difficult to stop or control the worry.  Subtypes: Failure to Control, Intrusive Worry, Chaining of Worry\n[S058] Restlessness_Tension: Restlessness, or feeling keyed up or on edge.  Subtypes: Feeling Keyed Up, Physical Tension, Nervousness\n[S059] Concentration_Difficulty: Difficulty concentrating or mind going blank.  Subtypes: Interference by Worry, Inability to Sustain Attention, Mind Going Blank\n[S060] Irritability: A state of reacting sensitively or sharply to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Low Frustration Tolerance, Snappiness, Exhausted Patience\n[S061] Muscle_Tension: A state where muscles are continuously tense, stiff, and accompanied by discomfort.  Subtypes: Neck and Shoulder Tension, Jaw Tension, Generalized Muscle Aches, Chronic Stiffness\n[S062] Specific_Phobic_Fear: Marked fear or anxiety about a specific object or situation that is out of proportion to the actual danger. Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others. Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health. I mean, I’ve definitely had stretches where I’ve been worn down or discouraged, especially when my mind keeps circling back to the ensemble situation, but I don’t think there’s been a two-week period where I was just… persistently hopeless, or couldn’t take interest in anything at all.\n\nI still get up, run, work, listen to music, cook dinner with my wife, that sort of thing. Sometimes the enjoyment is a little contaminated by the background noise in my head, if that makes sense, but it isn’t gone.\n\nAnd on the other end, I don’t think I’ve had anything like being unusually euphoric or unstoppable. I can have busy periods where I’m very absorbed in work and my thoughts move quickly because I’m trying to solve a composition problem, but that feels like my normal working mode, not some dramatic shift where I don’t need sleep or I’m suddenly full of grand plans or anything. So… no, not really. At least not in the way you’re asking.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Anticipatory Anxiety, Immediate Panic Response, Physiological Panic Symptoms, Freezing\n[S064] Phobic_Avoidance_Endure: The phobic object or situation is actively avoided or endured with intense fear or anxiety.  Subtypes: Active Avoidance, Functional Impairment due to Avoidance, Enduring with Distress, Reliance on Safety Signals\n[S065] Obsessions: Recurrent and persistent thoughts, urges, or images that are experienced as intrusive and unwanted, and attempts to ignore or suppress them.  Subtypes: Contamination Obsessions, Pathological Doubt, Symmetry and Exactness Obsessions, Forbidden or Taboo Thoughts, Somatic Obsessions\n[S066] Compulsions: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to an obsession or to prevent anxiety/distress; clearly excessive or not realistically connected.  Subtypes: Cleaning or Washing Compulsions, Checking Compulsions, Ordering and Arranging Compulsions, Mental Acts, Repeating Rituals\n[S067] Preoccupation_with_Appearance: Preoccupation with one or more perceived defects or flaws in physical appearance that are not observable or appear slight to others.  Subtypes: Skin Defects, Facial Features, Asymmetry, Muscle Dysmorphia, Specific Body Parts\n[S068] Appearance_Related_Repetitive_Behaviors: Repetitive behaviors or mental acts performed in response to appearance concerns.  Subtypes: Mirror Checking or Avoidance, Excessive Camouflaging, Skin Picking, Reassurance Seeking, Comparing, Seeking Cosmetic Procedures\n[S069] Intrusive_Symptoms: Recurrent, involuntary, and intrusive distressing memories of the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Intrusive Memories, Distressing Dreams, Dissociative Reactions (Flashbacks), Psychological Distress at Cues, Physiological Reactions to Cues\n[S070] Trauma_Avoidance: Persistent avoidance of stimuli associated with the traumatic event.  Subtypes: Internal Avoidance, External Avoidance\n[S071] Trauma_Related_Amnesia: Inability to remember an important aspect of the traumatic event (typically due to dissociative amnesia and not to other factors like head injury or alcohol).  Subtypes: Fragmented Memory, Generalized Amnesia\n[S072] Negative_Beliefs: Persistent and exaggerated negative beliefs or expectations about oneself, others, or the world.  Subtypes: Negative Self-Beliefs, Negative Beliefs about Others, Negative Worldview\n[S073] Distorted_Cognitions_With_Blame: Persistent, distorted cognitions about the cause or consequences of the traumatic event that lead the individual to blame himself/herself or others.  Subtypes: Self-Blame, Blaming Others\n[S074] Persistent_Negative_Emotional_State: Persistent negative emotional state (e.g., fear, horror, anger, guilt, or shame).  Subtypes: Persistent Fear, Persistent Horror, Persistent Anger, Persistent Guilt, Persistent Shame\n[S075] Social_Detachment: Feelings of detachment or estrangement from others.  Subtypes: Emotional Detachment, Social Isolation, Difficulty with Intimacy, Loss of Belonging\n[S076] Inability_Positive_Emotions: Persistent inability to experience positive emotions (e.g., inability to experience happiness, satisfaction, or loving feelings).  Subtypes: Anhedonia for Happiness, Lack of Satisfaction, Inability to Feel Love, Emotional Numbness\n[S077] Irritability_Aggression: Irritable behavior and angry outbursts (with little or no provocation) typically expressed as verbal or physical aggression.  Subtypes: Impulsive Anger, Verbal Aggression, Physical Aggression, Chronic Irritability\n[S078] Reckless_Self_Destructive_Behavior: Reckless or self-destructive behavior pattern that endangers one's safety or health.  Subtypes: Risky Behaviors, Substance Abuse, Indisicriminate Sexual Behavior, Self-Harm\n[S079] Hypervigilance: Persistent state of being alert and tense about the surrounding environment.  Subtypes: Scanning for Danger, Safety Checking, Sustained Alertness\n[S080] Exaggerated_Startle_Response: Exaggerated startle response to minor stimuli.  Subtypes: Auditory Startle, Tactile Startle, Visual Startle, Slow Recovery\n[S081] Stress_Related_Maladaptive_Response: Emotional or behavioral symptoms appearing in response to an identifiable stressor.  Subtypes: With Depressed Mood, With Anxiety, With Mixed Anxiety and Depressed Mood, With Disturbance of Conduct, With Mixed Disturbance of Emotions and Conduct, Unspecified\n[S082] Depersonalization_Derealization: Altered sense of the reality of one's self or surroundings after a traumatic event.  Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nIt’s been there since after I won the commission and was invited to conduct the premiere, so… not quite two full years, more like a year and nine months. That’s when it really started to take shape in my mind.\n\nI wouldn’t say it’s disappeared for any real stretch of time since then. There are moments when I’m distracted or busy enough that I’m not actively thinking about it every second, but the belief itself hasn’t gone away. It’s more like it moves into the background and then comes forward again, especially when something reminds me of the ensemble or that whole situation. So yes, in that sense it’s been continuous since it started.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate. Err toward UNKNOWN (omit) rather than guessing.\n\nReply ONLY with valid JSON (no markdown, no extra text):\n{\n  \"confirmed\": [\"S001\", ...],\n  \"denied\": [\"S025\", ...],\n  \"reasoning\": {\n    \"S001\": \"one-sentence justification\",\n    ...\n  }\n}"
  }
]

Subtypes: Depersonalization, Derealization, Time Distortion\n\n=== PATIENT UTTERANCE ===\nI mean… I hear what you’re asking. And I know how it sounds when I say that the emails don’t really settle it for me. But no, the certainty doesn’t really go away.\n\nWhen someone shows me a scheduling note or says, “See, this was just a room conflict,” or “That seating change was already planned,” I can understand the words. I’m not saying I can’t read the email or that I don’t see the innocent explanation they’re offering. It’s just… it feels like an explanation after the fact, or like it covers one tiny piece while leaving the larger pattern untouched.\n\nBecause it wasn’t just one schedule change, you know? It was the way the schedule changes happened after the commission, and then the seating choices, and the little comments in rehearsal that could be brushed off as nothing if you looked at each one separately. That’s what people keep doing—they isolate one thing and say, “Well, here’s an email explaining this.” But to me that doesn’t answer the whole thing.\n\nSo, is there a part of me that tries to consider I might be wrong? Maybe briefly, in an intellectual sense, because I know my wife and other people want me to consider that. I can sort of say, “Fine, maybe this one email is legitimate.” But it doesn’t shake the overall certainty. I still come back to the same conclusion: that a few people in the ensemble were quietly undermining my work. And honestly, that’s part of why I’m here, because I can’t seem to shake that, even when people keep showing me things that are supposed to explain it away.\n\nTask:\n- CONFIRMED: symptoms clearly present or strongly implied by the patient's words.\n- DENIED: symptoms the patient explicitly says they do NOT have\n  (e.g. \"No\", \"I don't\", \"I haven't\", \"never\").\n- Do NOT speculate.
